| e                               | E                               | é                               | É                               | peče                            | vs             | péče           |
+---------------------------------+---------------------------------+---------------------------------+---------------------------------+---------------------------------+----------------+----------------+
| i / y                           | I / Y                           | í / ý                           | Í / Ý                           | lyže                            | vs             | líže           |
+---------------------------------+---------------------------------+---------------------------------+---------------------------------+---------------------------------+----------------+----------------+
| o                               | O                               | ó                               | Ó                               | boje                            | vs             | bóje           |
+---------------------------------+---------------------------------+---------------------------------+---------------------------------+---------------------------------+----------------+----------------+
| u                               | U                               | ú / ů                           | Ú                               | muže                            | vs             | může           |
+---------------------------------+---------------------------------+---------------------------------+---------------------------------+---------------------------------+----------------+----------------+

### Pronouncing syllables, words and sentences

This section describes what happens when we go beyond the pronunciation
of individual sounds.

#### End of word devoicing

All consonants at the end of a word are pronounced unvoiced. This is
often only in one or two of their declined or conjugated forms. When a
word is spelled with a voiced consonant at the end, there are other
forms (for instance including a vowel) where it is pronouced voiced.
Here are some common patterns for nouns and verbs.

##### Devoiced nominative masculine singular

Many masculine nouns (of all models) end in a voiced consonant which is
devoiced in the nominative singular and voiced in all other forms where
an ending is added.

  -------------------------------------------
  **Masc Sg**             **Masc Pl**
  ----------------------- -------------------
  hra[d]{.underline}      hra**d**y

  kamará[d]{.underline}   kamará**d**i

  sta[v]{.underline}      sta**v**y

  bato[h]{.underline}     bato**h**y

  du[b]{.underline}       du**b**y

  mrá[z]{.underline}      mra**z**y

  mu[ž]{.underline}       mu**ž**i
  -------------------------------------------

##### Devoiced feminine genitive plural

Many femine nouns of the *voda* model have a voiced consonant before the
final *a*. They are devoiced in the *Plural Genitive*.

  -----------------------------------------------------------
  **Fem Sg**                    **Fem Gen Pl**
  ----------------------------- -----------------------------
  vo**d**a                      vo[d]{.underline}

  ra**d**a                      ra[d]{.underline}

  krá**v**a                     kra[v]{.underline}

  vá**h**a                      va[h]{.underline}

  do**b**a                      do[b]{.underline}

  vá**z**a                      vá[z]{.underline}
  -----------------------------------------------------------

##### Devoiced impetatives

In some models, the final consonant in the imperative is devoiced.

  ----------------------------------------------------------
  **Verb** (3^rd^ Sg)         **Imperative** (Sg)
  --------------------------- ------------------------------
  ra**d**í                    ra[ď]{.underline}

  ří**d**í                    ři[ď]{.underline}

  se**d**í                    se[ď]{.underline}

  pla**v**e                   pla[v]{.underline}

  chlu**b**í                  chlu[b]{.underline}

  le**z**e                    le[z]{.underline}

  le**ž**í                    le[ž]{.underline}
  ----------------------------------------------------------

##### Devoicing homophones

Final consonant devoicing results in a number of homofones. However,
they rarely result in true ambiguity because they appear across parts of
speech or case. While devoiced homophones do not cause misunderstanding,
they are a frequent source of spelling erorrs for native speakers. They
are also often used for word play.

  -----------------------------------------------
  **Unvoiced stem**       **Devoiced homophone**
  ----------------------- -----------------------
  dup *(stomp)*           dub *(oak)*

  let *(flight, of        led *(ice)*
  years)*                 

  chlup *(a hair          chlub *(boast!)*
  \[animal\])*            

  řiť *(anus)*            řiď *(drive!)*

  les *(forest)*          lez *(crawl!)*

  zeť *(son in law)*      zeď *(wall)*
  -----------------------------------------------

#### Voicing assimilation

There are a few things that happen to individual sounds when put
together. Especially consonants are vulnerable. If two consonants meet,
the second always makes the first change its voicedness.[^1] For
instance, in *kde* the voiced [d]{.underline} changes the unvoiced
[k]{.underline} so that it is pronounced as voiced [g]{.underline}. The
pause is unvoiced so ending consonants are always pronounced unvoiced.
Additionally, the following word always forces the change on the final
consonant of the preceding word. Voiced consonants without an unvoiced
pair (i.e. *m, n, l, r, j*) and *v *do not usually trigger assimilation.

##### Feminine genitive plural feminine and neuter

When the final vowel in a feminine or neuter model is preceded by two
consonants, the first consonant is revoiced in Genitive Plural.

  --------------------------------------------------
  **Sg**                    **Gen Pl**
  ------------------------- ------------------------
  tu[ž]{.underline}ka       tu**ž**ek

  kamará[d]{.underline}ka   kamará**d**ek

  dru[ž]{.underline}ka      dru**ž**ek

  hro[b]{.underline}ka      hro**b**ek

  slů[v]{.underline}ko      slů**v**ek

  lů[ž]{.underline}ko       lů**ž**ek
  --------------------------------------------------

##### *k* \> *g* voicing

When *k *is followed by a voiced consonant, it assimilates to the voiced
*g*. Because non-assimilated *g* is almost always associated with loan
words, Czechs do not realize that this is happening and will claim that
they in fact pronounce *k.*

This voicing assimilation happens within as well as between words. It is
most prominent in the very frequent wh- question words: *kde, kdo, kdy*
which are pronounced *gde, gdo, gdy.*

The final *k *in *jak* (how) is often voiced as *g* when followed by a
voiced consonant. For instance, *jak do* (how to) is pronounced *jagdo*.

#### *ř* devoicing

*ř* can also be devoiced. Voiceless *ř* is not an independent sound in
Czech and most Czechs have no awareness of its existence. The voiced *ř*
is unique to Czech but the voiceless *ř* can be found in Polish and
other languages.

Devoiced *ř* appears in two contexts:

1.  In a consonant cluster where *ř* is the final consontant preceded by
    a voiceless consonant.

2.  When *ř* is the final consonant (usually in imperatives):

Most common patterns with devoiced *ř*:

  ---------------------------------------------------------------------
  Pattern                  **Examples**
  ------------------------ --------------------------------------------
  kř                       *křída, křest, křen, křesťan, skřítek*

  př                       *před, přes, příklad, přítel, přát (si),
                           spříst*

  tř                       *tři, třída, třeba, třít, třást (se), střed,
                           střelit, střep*

  -ř                       *měř, miř, věř, zvěř*
  ---------------------------------------------------------------------

#### Consonant Clusters

Another prominent feature of Czech pronunciation is its tendency to
cluster consonants. As many as 4 consonants can be in one syllable
without any vowels. Thus, *zmrzlina* (icecream) is in fact a t-syllable
word. Examples of monosyllabic words without any vowels are *smrt*,
*krk, vlk, smrk, pštros*, *strom, stvol*.

6 consonants in a row can be found in verbs in the past tense such as
*scvrkl* (shrank) forming two syllables and even a 8 consonant rarity
could be contemplated in the verb *scvrnkls* (you flicked \[sth\] off a
surface). There is also the famous Czech sentence *Strč prst skrz krk*
which seemingly lacks vowels completely because *r* plays the role of
the vowel.

#### Syllables

When you speak, it is not really the sounds you pronounce, it is
*syllables, words and sentences*. Thus, when you practice pronunciation
start putting the sounds into syllables and words. You will also find
that then they are easier to pronounce.

All syllables have to have at least a vowel or a sound that plays the
role of a vowel. In Czech, *r* and *l* can play the role of a vowel
which is why it is possible to have Czech syllables with just consonants
such as *krk* or *vlk*. It is possible to have have a syllable with just
a vowel. In Czech, there are even two words *a* and *i* that are just a
vowel. But it is not possible to have a syllable with just one
consonant.

Each language has a typical syllable patterns, or combinations of vowels
and consonants (VC). In Czech, these patterns are most typical.

  -------- -------------- -------------------------------------
  CV       50%            na, se, já, máma, Lída, kolo, jede,
                          koruna

  CVC      15%            rok, sůl, nad, sám, Radka, krk, plk

  CCV      10%            sto, pro,

  CCVC     8%             proč, krok, strk, smrk, zmrzlina

  CCCVC                   strom

  CCCCVC                  pštros

                          
  -------- -------------- -------------------------------------

Most words you find in text, will have three or four syllables. This is
partly because most words have an ending added to them which often adds
a syllable. Two syllable words are common but single syllable word are
relatively rare. Mostly pronouns and prepositions.

#### Stress

Syllables are what carries stress. In Czech, it is always the **first
syllable** that is stressed. Each sentence is divided into stress
segments.

> \| **Mám** \| **rád** \| **o**vocné \| **kned**líky. \|
>
> \| **Do**brý \| **u**čitel \| **má** \| **rád** \| **své** \|
> **stu**denty \|

Notice, that the **first syllable** (in bold) is always stressed even
when the vowel is short and the vowel in the following syllable is long.
The **unstressed vowels** maintain their quality and are not reduced.
This is very different from English. In English, only the stresed vowel
is pronounced fully, the others are reduced.

Also, the stress segments are of **different lengths**. In English, the
space between two stressed syllables is usually the same. English is a
stress-timed language which means that vowels in unstressed syllables
are reduced to keep the space between stresses roughly the same. For
example, "give me a call" becomes "gimmeuh call" or "I'm going to"
becomes "amana". That's how knock knock jokes work. Czech is syllable
timed and the space between stressed syllables is more variable. This
gives it a different rhythm.

These two features make it harder (but not impossible) to rap in Czech.
But most importantly, they make it very challenging for English speakers
to sound natural. This is the **most important feature of Czech**
**pronunciation** to learn well.

Usually, one word occupies one stress segment but sometimes words
combine in a way that the stress shifts between them. Usually,
prepositions and some particles like *mi, mu, si* or *se* are combined
with another word to be in a stress segment.

> \| **Uči**li se \| **čes**ky\| **od** rána \| **do** noci. \|
>
> \| **Mě**la jsem mu \| **rá**no \| **za**volat. \|

#### Intonation

##### Intonation Rules

There are basically two types of intonation in Czech:

- **simply descending** which is used for simple statements and
  questions with question word

- **flat, rising on the last syllable** which is used for YES/NO
  questions

  ------- ------------------------------------------------------------------
  **!**   No matter what the intonation pattern, the stress is always on the
          first syllable of every word which gives the intonation a slight
          rising pattern on each word.

  ------- ------------------------------------------------------------------

##### Statement intonation

+------+------+------+------+
| **\**                     |
| Petr jede domů.           |
+------+------+------+------+
| pe   |      |      |      |
+------+------+------+------+
|      | tr   |      |      |
+------+------+------+------+
|      |      | je   |      |
+------+------+------+------+
|      |      |      | de   |
+------+------+------+------+
|      |      |      |      |
+------+------+------+------+
|      |      |      |      |
+------+------+------+------+

+-------+-------+-------+-------+-------+-------+
| Mám se dobře.                                 |
+---------------+---------------+---------------+
| mám           |               |               |
+-------+-------+-------+-------+-------+-------+
|       | se            |               |       |
+-------+---------------+---------------+-------+
|       |               | dob           |       |
+-------+---------------+---------------+-------+
|       |               |               | ře    |
+-------+---------------+---------------+-------+

#####  Wh- Question Intonation 

+--------+--------+--------+
| Jak se máš?              |
+--------+--------+--------+
| jak    |        |        |
+--------+--------+--------+
|        | se     |        |
+--------+--------+--------+
|        |        | máš    |
+--------+--------+--------+

+-------+-------+-------+-------+
| Co děláš?                     |
+-------+-------+-------+-------+
| co    |       |       |       |
+-------+-------+-------+-------+
|       | dě    |       |       |
+-------+-------+-------+-------+
|       |       | láš   |       |
+-------+-------+-------+-------+

+-----+-----+-----+-----+
| Co dělá Petr?         |
+-----+-----+-----+-----+
| co  |     |     |     |
+-----+-----+-----+-----+
|     | dě  |     |     |
+-----+-----+-----+-----+
|     |     | lá  |     |
+-----+-----+-----+-----+
|     |     |     | pe  |
+-----+-----+-----+-----+
|     |     |     |     |
+-----+-----+-----+-----+

##### YES/NO Question Intonation

+------+------+------+------+------+
| Jede Petr domů?                  |
+------+------+------+------+------+
|      |      |      |      |      |
+------+------+------+------+------+
| je   | de   | pe   | tr   | do   |
+------+------+------+------+------+

+------+------+------+
| Máš se dobře?      |
+------+------+------+
|      |      |      |
+------+------+------+
| máš  | se   | dob  |
+------+------+------+

## Spelling

Czech spelling is reputed to be very regular. It is true in that it is
learnable. But it is not completely straightforward. The are basically
three types of rules that are at work.

1.  **Letter = sound**: a certain combination is always pronounced in a
    certain way. For instance, if you see *á* you pronounce it as
    aaaargh.

2.  **Letter combinations** are pronounced in a certain way. For
    instance, ě in m[ě]{.underline} is pronounced differently than when
    it follows v[ě]{.underline}, or d[ě]{.underline}.

3.  **Assimilation of consonants** described in the chapter on
    Pronunciation

4.  **Exceptions:** Some words or patterns of words are known to be
    spelled differently from the rules 1-3. Most common with some uses
    of *i* vs *y*.

#### Czech accent marks - Diacritics

+-------+-----------+-------------------------------------------------+
| **´** | čárka     | used to lengthen vowels (the vowel is the same  |
|       |           | only twice as long)                             |
|       |           |                                                 |
|       |           | can be used with all vowel with the same effect |
+-------+-----------+-------------------------------------------------+
| **ˇ** | háček     | used to soften consonants *c, d, n, r, s, t, z* |
|       |           |                                                 |
|       |           | if *d, t, n* are followed by *e* the ˇ is       |
|       |           | placed over the *e*, when followed by *i/í* the |
|       |           | ˇ is dropped (see below)                        |
|       |           |                                                 |
|       |           | (**Note:** In print, háček ˇ over *d* and *t*   |
|       |           | is displayed as an apostrophe ': *ť, ď*.)       |
+-------+-----------+-------------------------------------------------+
| **°** | kroužek   | only used with *u* if it is not the first       |
|       |           | letter in the word; otherwise ´ is used. For    |
|       |           | instance, **ú**kol vs dom**ů** or s**ů**l       |
+-------+-----------+-------------------------------------------------+

#### Spelling rules for *i* and *ě*

+------------+-------+-------+-------+---------------------+---------------------------+
| **letter** | **+** | **letter**    | **is pronounced**   | **as in**                 |
+------------+-------+-------+-------+---------------------+---------------------------+
| n          | \+    | i     | í     | ňi / ňí             | nyní                      |
+------------+-------+-------+-------+---------------------+---------------------------+
| t          | \+    | i     | í     | ťi / ťí             | titíž                     |
+------------+-------+-------+-------+---------------------+---------------------------+
| d          | \+    | i     | í     | ďi / ďí             | jdi                       |
+------------+-------+-------+-------+---------------------+---------------------------+
| n          | \+    | ě     |       | ňe                  | něco                      |
+------------+-------+-------+-------+---------------------+---------------------------+
| d          | \+    | ě     |       | ťe                  | tělo                      |
+------------+-------+-------+-------+---------------------+---------------------------+
| t          | \+    | ě     |       | ďe                  | dělo                      |
+------------+-------+-------+-------+---------------------+---------------------------+
| m          | \+    | ě     |       | mně                 | město                     |
+------------+-------+-------+-------+---------------------+---------------------------+
| b/p/v      | \+    | ě     |       | bje / pje / vje     | věc                       |
+------------+-------+-------+-------+---------------------+---------------------------+

The above chart describes how *n, t, d, m, b, p, v* are pronounced when
followed by *ě* or *i/í*. Notice that the *ě* is pronounced as a regular
*e*.

#### Use of *y* vs. *i*

The distinction between ***Y*** and. ***I*** is the most irregular
phenomenon in the Czech spelling system. Both ***y*** and ***i*** denote
identical sounds. ***Y*** is used regularly after all the hard
consonants and never after soft consonants. Its use after ambiguous
consonants is completely arbitrary and the words which use ***y*** have
to be memorized by rote (even by native speakers.)

##### Basic rules of use of *i* and *y* after consonants

+-------+:----------------------------------------+:----------------------+
| **i\  | after **ž, š, c, č, ř, j, ď, ť, ň**     | děti, život, žít, jít |
| í**   |                                         |                       |
|       +-----------------------------------------+-----------------------+
|       | after **b, f, m , l , p, s, v, z** in   | bít, lid, dopis, vila |
|       | all words where **y** is not            |                       |
|       | specifically required                   |                       |
|       +-----------------------------------------+-----------------------+
|       | after **l** in past plural form of      | muži šli, Petr a Jana |
|       | verbs if the subject is *masculine      | šli (masc. takes      |
|       | animate*                                | precendence)          |
+-------+-----------------------------------------+-----------------------+
| **y** | after **h, ch, k, r, d, t, n**          | nohy, roky, ryba,     |
|       |                                         | rány                  |
| **ý** |                                         |                       |
|       +-----------------------------------------+-----------------------+
|       | after **b, f, m , l , p, s, v, z** for  | být, byt, mýt, lyže,  |
|       | prescribed words (can be found in Czech | sypat, vyjít, vykat,  |
|       | spelling manuals)                       | brzy, jazyk, ...      |
|       +-----------------------------------------+-----------------------+
|       | after ***l*** in past plural form of    | ženy a děti šly       |
|       | verbs if the subject is not *masculine  |                       |
|       | animate*                                |                       |
+-------+-----------------------------------------+-----------------------+

  ---- ------------------------------------------------------------------
  !    There are a few instances where *i* and *y* distinguish the
       meaning of two words. Most notably *bít* (to beat) vs *být* (to
       be) and *mít* (to have) vs *mýt* (to wash). In spoken language,
       however, these words are homonymous.

  ---- ------------------------------------------------------------------

#### *ú* vs. *ů*

The only difference is the position in the word.

+-------+------------------------------------+-----------------------------+
| **ú** | beginning of words                 | úkol, úspěch, účet,         |
|       +------------------------------------+-----------------------------+
|       | words where a prefix is added to   | účet - zúčtovat, úhel -     |
|       | the beginning of a word whose root | pravoúhlý                   |
|       | begins with **ú**                  |                             |
+-------+------------------------------------+-----------------------------+
| **ů** | middle and end of word             | různý, způsob, Petrů        |
+-------+------------------------------------+-----------------------------+

#### Using Capital Letters

Czech rules for the use of capital letters are different from English.
Titles of books and articles, and names of states and institutions all
capitalize only the first word. Only cities with two names capitalize
both. Also not capitalized are names of days of the week and months.
Even words like names of nations are not capitalized when used as
adjectives. Names of languages are also not capitalized.

#### Punctuation

Czech punctuation rules are much stricter then those of English.

+-------+-----------+:--------------------------+:-------------------+:------------------------+
| **,** | čárka     | After all conjunctions    | Neví to, protože je hloupý. Není tu, ale     |
|       |           | except ***a, nebo, i***.  | poslal peníze.                               |
|       |           +---------------------------+----------------------------------------------+
|       |           | Always to separate        | Protože neměl peníze, nepřišel.              |
|       |           | clauses.                  |                                              |
|       |           +---------------------------+----------------------------------------------+
|       |           | Always to separate        | Slíbil to, ale, protože neměl peníze,        |
|       |           | inserts longer than one   | neudělal to.                                 |
|       |           | word.                     |                                              |
+-------+-----------+---------------------------+----------------------------------------------+
| **;** | středník  | Rarely used in Czech texts, Czech typewriter keyboard doesn\'t even      |
|       |           | include it.                                                              |
+-------+-----------+--------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| **:** | dvojtečka | Similar to English.                                                      |
+-------+-----------+--------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| **?** | otazník   | Similar to English.                                                      |
+-------+-----------+------------------------------------------------+-------------------------+
| **!** | vykřičník | Used always at the end of imperative           | *Jdi domů!* (Go home!)  |
|       |           | sentences.                                     |                         |
|       |           |                                                |                         |
|       |           | Not usually used for exclamations.             |                         |
+-------+-----------+------------------------------------------------+-------------------------+

## Idioms, Phraseology and Vocabulary

This section describes the very important parts of language that usually
fall somewhere between the grammar, dictionary and the phrasebook. You
rarely find them in one place. But without them, you can never consider
yourself to be proficient in a language.

The list here is not exhaustive and is presented from the point of view
of an English speaking learner. We focused on things that are different
from English and a common point of confusion. However, they are real
patterns and learners coming from any language can benefit from them.

This section is forever incomplete. There is a long list of subsections
in progress that may appear in future editions.

### Words with a difference

This section is particularly English centric. It describes words the
usage of which is in some special way different from English. Speakers
of other languages may find them unremarkable. These differences go both
ways, often speakers of Czech will make mistakes in English because of
confusions arising from these words.

#### ask: zeptat se vs. požádat

There are two very distinct ways of asking. One is asking for
information and the other is asking for a favor. This translates into
two Czech words:

+:---------------------------------+:----------------------------------+
| **zeptat se**                    | **požádat**                       |
+----------------------------------+-----------------------------------+
| Zeptal se kolik je hodin.\       | Požádal ho, aby odešel.           |
| (*He asked what time it was.*)   |                                   |
|                                  | (*He asked him to leave.*)        |
+----------------------------------+-----------------------------------+

**Note:** Often, *říct* \[*tell to*\] will be used instead of *požádat*.
*They asked him to leave.* will be translated as *Řekli mu aby odešel*.
\[They told him to leave.\]

#### How to say Czech in Czech

Where there is one word in English there are five in Czech.

  ----------------------------- -----------------------------------------
  čeština                       Czech language

  Češka                         Czech woman

  Čech                          Czech man

  český, česká, české           Czech (adj.) as in Czech Republic

  česky                         Czech as in *I speak Czech* - an adverb
  ----------------------------- -----------------------------------------

Compare this with the words for English!

+----------------+----------------------------------+----------------------------------------+
| angličtina                                        | English language                       |
+---------------------------------------------------+----------------------------------------+
| Angličanka                                        | English woman                          |
+---------------------------------------------------+----------------------------------------+
| Angličan                                          | English man                            |
+---------------------------------------------------+----------------------------------------+
| anglický, anglická, anglické                      | English (tea)                          |
+---------------------------------------------------+----------------------------------------+
| anglicky                                          | English as in *I speak English* - an   |
|                                                   | adverb                                 |
+----------------+----------------------------------+----------------------------------------+
| !              | Note that only the names of nationalities are spelled with a capital      |
|                | letter.                                                                   |
+----------------+---------------------------------------------------------------------------+

#### to cut in Czech

  -----------------------------------------------------------------------
                         to (verb) (ipf, pf)    a (noun)
  ---------------------- ---------------------- -------------------------
  cut (with scissors)    stříhat, střihnout     střih

  cut (with an ax)       sekat, seknout         sek

  cut (with a saw,       řezat, říznout         řez
  knife)                                        
  -----------------------------------------------------------------------

#### *First* in Czech

The word *first* has two equivalents in Czech in different contexts.

  -------------------------- ------------------ -------------------------
  First from a group.        Přišel             He arrived ***first***.
                             **první*.***       

  First in a sequence of     **Nejdříve**       ***First**,* I have
  activities.                snídám.            breakfast.
  -------------------------- ------------------ -------------------------

#### function/work

To describe something as functioning, Czech uses the metaphor of motion
and not of work. Therefore, the word *pracovat* can never be used in
this context. Here are some examples:

  ---------------------- ------------------------ -----------------------
  **fungovat**           **jít/chodit**           **běžet**

  Motor funguje.\        Nejde mi počítač.\       Naše televize už zase
  (The engine works.)    (My computer doesn't     běží. (Out TV works
                         work.)                   again.)
  ---------------------- ------------------------ -----------------------

#### *Going* on foot and by vehicle

Where English has one word meaning *to go,* Czech has two. One for
*going on foot,* and second for *going by a vehicle* (car, bike, train,
bus).

+:-------------------------------------------:+:-------------------------------------------:+:-----------------------------------:+:-----------------------------------:+
| ![Walk](media/image89.svg){width="0.7903226159230097in"    | ![Car](media/image91.svg){width="1.0in"    |
| height="0.7903226159230097in"}                                                            | height="1.0in"}                                                           |
+-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+---------------------------------------------------------------------------+
| **jít**                                                                                   | **jet**                                                                   |
+---------------------------------------------+---------------------------------------------+-------------------------------------+-------------------------------------+
| jdu                                         | jdeme                                       | jedu                                | jedeme                              |
+---------------------------------------------+---------------------------------------------+-------------------------------------+-------------------------------------+
| jdeš                                        | jdete                                       | jedeš                               | jedete                              |
+---------------------------------------------+---------------------------------------------+-------------------------------------+-------------------------------------+
| jde                                         | jdou                                        | jede                                | jedou                               |
+---------------------------------------------+---------------------------------------------+-------------------------------------+-------------------------------------+

  ---------- -------------------------------- ---------------------------
  **jít**    pěšky                            on foot

  **jet**    vlakem, autem, autobusem, na     by train, car, bus; on a
             kole, na koni                    bike, horse
  ---------- -------------------------------- ---------------------------

The same difference shows up in other verbs of motion as well. For
example: *nést* (carry in hand) vs. *vézt* (carry by vehicle).

#### funny, happy, lucky vs. veselý, šťastný, směšný

There are many words in Czech to match the English funny, happy and
lucky, but none of them are a perfect match.

  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- ------------------
  funny (laughable) směšný            happy             šťastný
                                      (optimistic)      

  funny (amusing)   zábavný           happy (cheerful)  veselý

  funny (strange)   podivný           to be lucky       mít štěstí
  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- ------------------

#### fun in Czech

There is no easy way to express the English word 'fun' in Czech, in all
its contexts. Here are some example phrases. They often use words like
*zábava* (entertainment), *legrace* (humour), *prča* (informal)

  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  We'll have fun.                     Bude zábava. Budeme se bavit.

  They're having fun.                 Dobře se baví.

  She is fun. She is a fun person.    Je s ní zábava. Je s ní legrace.
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------

#### to know in Czech

+-------+------------------+------------------+------------------+------------------+----------------------+
| **vědět**                | \+               | že \..., co      | Vím, kdo jsi.    | I know who you are.  |
|                          |                  | \..., kdo \...,  |                  |                      |
|                          |                  | kdy ..., etc.    | Víš, kde je      | Do you know where    |
|                          |                  |                  | Praha?           | Prague is?           |
|                          |                  |                  |                  |                      |
|                          |                  |                  | Víš, co to je?   | Do you know what it  |
|                          |                  |                  |                  | is?                  |
+--------------------------+------------------+------------------+------------------+----------------------+
| **znát**                 | \+               | Koho? Co?        | Znám pana Pavla. | I know Mr. Pavel.    |
|                          |                  |                  |                  |                      |
|                          |                  |                  | Znám Prahu.      | I know Prague.       |
|                          |                  |                  |                  |                      |
|                          |                  |                  | Znáš Janu? Znám  | Do you know Jana? I  |
|                          |                  |                  | ji.              | know her.            |
+--------------------------+------------------+------------------+------------------+----------------------+
| **umět**                 | \+               | infinitive       | Umím zpívat.     | I can sing.          |
|                          |                  |                  |                  |                      |
|                          |                  | (noun, adverb)   | Umíš hrát        | Can you play         |
|                          |                  |                  | košíkovou?       | basketball?          |
|                          |                  |                  |                  |                      |
|                          |                  |                  | Umíš česky?      | Do you speak Czech?  |
+-------+------------------+------------------+------------------+------------------+----------------------+
| !     | Sometimes, the infinitive after ***umět*** is only assumed, and ***umět*** is followed directly  |
|       | by a noun or an adverb. E.g. *Umím (vařit) polévku.* or *Umím (mluvit) česky.*                   |
+-------+--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+

#### Illnesses and treatments that are and that are not

Despite the advances in international medicine, there are still
interesting differences in the popular terminology of different
languages.

  ----------------- --------------- ---------------------------------------------
  **rýma**          *cold*          Czech cure for a stuffy nose is to blow your
                                    nose. Even in public. Americans prefer to
                                    sniff. Mutually socially unacceptable.

  **angína**        *tonsillitis*   Very common complaint for Czechs, often used
                                    for conditions not technically a tonsillitis
                                    but more of a soar throat.

  **angína          *angina*        Commonly confused in Czech-English
  pectoris**                        translations.

  **nachlazení**    *catch a chill* Not as important a complaint in English
                                    (hence no noun). Czech cure is no ice-cream,
                                    keep warm and prevent drafts (*průvan*).

  **obklad**        *compress*      Common Czech cure for soar throat, headache,
                                    sprains, etc.

  **pálení žáhy**   *heartburn*     Seems slightly more common in English.

  **sádra**         *plaster cast*  Seems to be a slightly more popular cure for
                                    broken and sprained limbs.

  **anestetikum**   *novocaine*     Used only sparingly (for most serious
                                    operations) by Czech dentists. Brand as such
                                    not known to Czechs.
  ----------------- --------------- ---------------------------------------------

#### Jaký vs. Který / What *vs.* Which

This is a very easy distinction which is also infinitely tricky. The two
Czech words correspond almost exactly to two English words. But in
English *what* can often be used instead of which, which is very
confusing when translated directly into Czech.

+-----------+:------------------+:-----------------+:---------------------+
| **jaký**  | Jaký chceš        | What roll do you | expresses quality    |
|           | rohlík?           | want.            | independent of       |
|           |                   |                  | surroundings         |
|           | Chci dobrý        | I want a good    |                      |
|           | rohlík.           | roll.            |                      |
+-----------+-------------------+------------------+----------------------+
| **který** | Který rohlík      | Which roll do    | chooses one out of a |
|           | chceš?            | you want?        | group of many        |
|           |                   |                  |                      |
|           | Chci ten nalevo.  | I want the one   |                      |
|           |                   | on the left.     |                      |
+-----------+-------------------+------------------+----------------------+

#### Jak vs. Jaký / Adverbs vs. Adjectives

This is an important distinction, which is also expressed in English,
although not so strictly.

  ------------------- ----------------------- -------------------- -------------------------
  [Jak]{.underline}   Mám se                  [Jaký]{.underline}   To je [český]{.underline}
  se máš?             [dobře]{.underline}.    je to banán?         banán.

  How are you?        I\'m                    [What]{.underline}   It is a
                      [well]{.underline}.     kind of banana is    [Czech]{.underline}
                                              it?                  banana.

  [Jak]{.underline}   Pracuju                 [Jaká]{.underline}   Moje práce je
  pracuješ?           [pomalu]{.underline}.   je tvoje práce.      [zajímavá]{.underline}.

  [How]{.underline}   I work                  What is your work    My work is interesting.
  do you work.        [slowly]{.underline}.   like?                
  ------------------- ----------------------- -------------------- -------------------------

#### *Ještě ne* -- Not yet vs. *Už ne* -- Not anymore

  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  **Ještě ne -- not yet**             **Už ne -- not anymore**

  Ještě to nevím. -- *I don't know it Už to nevím. -- *I don't know it
  yet.*                               anymore.*

  Ještě jsem tam nebyl. -- *I haven't Už jsem tam nebyl. -- *I was not
  been there yet.*                    there anymore.*

  Ještě tam nebudu. -- *I won't be    Už tam nebudu. -- *I won't be there
  there yet.*                         anymore.*
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------

#### Use of je/jsou -- is vs. there is

The Czech *je* has several uses that require slightly different
translations into English. The translation may depend on word order or
on the general meaning.

+:----------------------------------+:----------------------------------+
| **je = is, jsou = are**           | **je = there is, jsou = there     |
|                                   | are**                             |
+-----------------------------------+-----------------------------------+
| *Herec je ve filmu.\*             | *Ve filmu je herec.\*             |
| = The actor is in a film.         | = There is an actor in the film.  |
|                                   |                                   |
| *Děti jsou doma.\*                | *Doma jsou děti.\*                |
| = The children are at home.       | = There are children at home.     |
+-----------------------------------+-----------------------------------+

**Note:** The same principle applies in the future (*there will
be/bude*) and past (*there was/byl*).

#### *Ještě* -- Still vs. *Už* -- Already

Although relatively simple, these two words can cause quite a few
problems. Compare their usage in negative and positive sentences. Notice
how the meaning of *už* changes from past to present. With the future
tense *ještě* and *už* can substitute for the future perfect in English,
as in *When you come, it will have been finished.* -- *Až přijdete, už
to bude hotové.* Unlike their English equivalents, both *ještě* and *už*
always appear in the beginning of the sentence.

  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  **Ještě -- still**                  **Už -- already, finally**

  Ještě jsem tady. -- *I'm still      Už jsem tady. -- *I'm finally
  here.*                              here.*

  Ještě jsem tam byl. -- *I was still Už jsem tam byl. -- *I was already
  there.*                             there.*

  Ještě tam budu. -- *I will still be Už tam budu. -- *I will be there
  there.*                             already.*
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------

#### Last in Czech - minulý vs poslední

Czech has two words to express the equivalent of the English last.

+--------------+:------------------+:--------------------------------+
| **poslední** | *last* in a       | *Byl poslední.* - He finished   |
|              | series            | last.                           |
|              +-------------------+---------------------------------+
|              | *latest* event    | *poslední móda* - latest        |
|              |                   | fashion                         |
+--------------+-------------------+---------------------------------+
| **minulý**   | *past* event      | *minulý rok* - last year        |
|              |                   |                                 |
|              |                   | *minulý* týden - last week      |
|              |                   |                                 |
|              |                   | *minulý čas* - past tense       |
+--------------+-------------------+---------------------------------+

#### make vs. do vs. work

Most of the uses of the English make are translated by *dělat/udělat*
into Czech. Only the sense of actually manufacturing something will use
the verb *vyrábět/vyrobit*.

+:---------------------+:---------------------+:-----------------------+
| **dělat/udělat\**    | **pracovat**\        | **vyrábět/vyrobit\**   |
| (do, make, work)     | (work)               | (make)                 |
+----------------------+----------------------+------------------------+
| Udělám dnes večer    | Pracuju v kanceláři. | Petr musel tu          |
| jídlo.\              |                      | součástku vyrobit.     |
| (I will make dinner  | (I work in an        |                        |
| tonight.)            | office.)             | (Petr had to make that |
|                      |                      | part.)                 |
+----------------------+----------------------+------------------------+

#### meet: potkat vs. setkat se

You can either meet someone by appointment or by accident. Czech has two
separate words for this. *Potkat* means to meet by accident and *setkat
se* means to meet by prior arrangement. *Setkat* *se* can also be
expressed as *mít schůzku* (to have a meeting, appointment).

  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  **potkat**                          **setkat se**

  Petr potkal Janu na ulici.\         Setkali se s prezidentem v deset
  (*Petr met/bumped into Jana in the  hodin.\
  street.*)                           (*They met the president at 10
                                      o'clock.*)
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------

#### na vs. v

The prototypical meaning of *na* is *on* while the prototypical meaning
of *v* is *in*. For a variety of places, though, *na* is used instead of
*v* with the same meaning. For example, *na Moravě.*

  ----------------------- -----------------------------------------------
  **countries that are    Gibraltar, Hawaii, Island, Nový Zéland (but
  islands or peninsulas** **not** Austrálie, Grónsko, Británie, Irsko,
                          Itálie or Španělsko)

  **selected other        Morava, Slovensko, Ukrajina, Podkarpatská Rus
  countries**             

  **local places on top   Vinohrady in Prague, or Kladno near Prague
  of hills**              (people from outside the region may use *v*)

  **some institutions**   pošta, policie, univerzita, fakulta, gymnázium

  **selected places**     náměstí, diskotéka, výstava, nádraží

  **activities (as        výlet, procházka, opera, hra, film
  destinations)**         
  ----------------------- -----------------------------------------------

#### Translating *no* into Czech

The English word *no* has two different meanings which require two
different words in Czech.

+-----------------------------------+-----------------------------------+
| ***no* as *ne***                  | **no as žádný**                   |
+-----------------------------------+-----------------------------------+
| Máte chleba? Ne, nemáme.          | Žádný student nenapsal test.      |
|                                   |                                   |
| (Do you have bread? No, we        | (No student wrote the test.)      |
| don't.)                           |                                   |
+-----------------------------------+-----------------------------------+

#### Use of *to* (this vs. that)

The Czech *to* can be confusing because it can be translated as two
different words:

+:----------------------------------+:---------------------------------+
| **to = this, that**               | **to = it**                      |
+-----------------------------------+----------------------------------+
| *To je problém.* = This/that is a | *Bylo to dobré.* = It was good.  |
| problem.                          |                                  |
|                                   | *Kdo je to?* = Who is it?.       |
| *To je Karel.* = This/that is     |                                  |
| Karel .                           |                                  |
+-----------------------------------+----------------------------------+

Sometimes, it is difficult to decide on a good translation.

#### there is/are in Czech

Czech doesn\'t have the construction *there is/there are*. Czech
speakers have many difficulties mastering this construction in English.

You simply use \'*je*\' or \'*jsou*\'. Sometimes it is possible to use
\'*má*\' or \'*mají*\' or even '*existuje*'.

+----------------------------------+-----------------------------------+
| there is a castle in Prague      | v Praze **je** hrad               |
+----------------------------------+-----------------------------------+
| there are problems in the USA    | v USA **jsou** problémy\          |
|                                  | v USA **mají** problémy\          |
|                                  | USA **mají** problémy             |
|                                  |                                   |
|                                  | v USA **existují** problémy       |
+----------------------------------+-----------------------------------+

**Note** that sometimes you must add \'tam\' or \'tady\', as in:

  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  there\'s a car over there           tam **je** auto\
                                      tam **máme/mají** auto

  there\'s a car here                 tady **je** auto
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------

### Idioms, set expressions and useful patterns

Idioms are expressions that are usually used as whole chunks in which
just a small part can be substituted. They also

#### Animal metaphors

  ---------- --------- ---------------------------------------------------
  lev        lion      hard working, strong

  rys        lynx      great eyes

  liška      fox       clever, crafty

  sova       owl       wise

  vlk        wolf      hungry

  myš        mouse     quiet
  ---------- --------- ---------------------------------------------------

#### Agreeing and disagreeing

  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- ------------------
  I (don't) agree.  (Ne)Souhlasím     Agreement         Shoda/Dohoda

  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- ------------------

#### Mental and Physical Health/Feelings *I am* vs. *it is* vs. *I have a*

There are many phrases that cannot be translated directly from English
to Czech. Often, groups of phrases have a similar translation. You need
to memorize which phrases translate in what way. Most commonly, the
literal translations oscillate between *to have/to be* or some other
verb.

##### I am = mám/nemám

  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- ------------------
  I'm thirsty.      Mám žízeň.        I'm hungry.       Mám hlad.

  I'm busy.         Nemám čas.        I'm               Mám čas/volno.
                                      free/available.   

  I'm lucky.        Mám štěstí.       I'm not lucky.    Mám smůlu.

  I'm happy/glad.   Mám radost.       I'm depressed.    Mám depresi.
  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- ------------------

##### I am = je mi

  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- ------------------
  *I'm cold.*       **Je mi zima.**   I'm warm.         Je mi teplo.

  I\'m sick.        Je mi špatně.     I'm well.         Je mi dobře.

  I'm sorry.        Je mi líto.\*     I'm sad.          Je mi smutno.
  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- ------------------

\* *I'm sorry* only translates as *je mi líto* in the context of
expressing regret and not in apologizing, where *promiňte*, or *pardon*
is used. Another equivalent is *mrzí mě*.

##### I have (a) = mám

  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- ------------------
  *I have a cold*   **Mám rýmu.**     I have a cough.   Mám kašel.

  *I have flu.*     **Mám chřipku.**  I have            Mám angínu.
                                      tonsillitis.      
  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- ------------------

##### I have a ...ache = bolí mě ...

  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- ------------------
  *I have a         **Bolí mě         I have a          Bolí mě zuby.
  headache.*        hlava.**          toothache.        

  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- ------------------

##### it is = je + adverb (weather)

  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- ------------------
  *It is cold.*     **Je zima.**      It's nice.        Je hezky.

  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- ------------------

##### I am = jsem

+----------------+----------------+----------------+-----------------+
| I'm glad.      | Jsem rád.      | I'm very       | Jsem šťastný.   |
|                |                | happy.         |                 |
|                |                |                | Jsem veselý.    |
+----------------+----------------+----------------+-----------------+

##### I am = a verb

  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- ------------------
  I\'m bored.       Nudím se.         I'm in a hurry.   Spěchám.

  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- ------------------

#### Expressing Possibility and Necessity

Similarly to English, both of these can be expressed through modal
verbs. For example,

  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  **Possibility/Impossibility**       **Necessity/Lack of necessity**

  lze, nelze                          třeba, potřeba, zapotřebí

  možno, možné                        nutno, nutné

  asi                                 nezbytné, nevyhnutné,
                                      neodvratitelné
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------

**Note:** *možná, třeba* are also used as *maybe*.

#### Incorrect idioms resulting from confusion with English

Very often, what you say in English does not sound very Czech when
translated literally. Here are a few examples of the more common
mistranslations of English phrases.

  ------------------- ---------------------- ----------------------------
  No problem.         Není problém.          To je v pořádku.

  Is it OK?           Je to dobré?           Nevadí to?

  How are you? So-so. Jak se máte? Tak-tak.  Jde to. Docela dobře.

  I am happy.         Jsem šťastný.          Mám radost. Je mi dobře.
                                             Jsem veselý.

  I have a question.  Mám otázku.            Můžu se zeptat?
  ------------------- ---------------------- ----------------------------

### Social interactions

#### Arranging appointments

  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- ------------------
  We'll meet at...  Sejdeme se v/u... I will drop in    Stavím se později.
                                      later.            

  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- ------------------

#### Asking questions with negatives

Czechs often ask negative questions to show politeness where English
would use *would* or just ask a direct question. For instance, ***Nevíte
kde je tady nádraží?***, ***Není tady pan Novák?***, ***Nemáte
housky?*** = Do you know where the train station is? Is Mr. Novák here?
Do you have housky?

  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  Nevíte, kde je nádraží?             Where is the train station, please?

  Nevíte, kolik je hodin?             Would you know what time it is?

  Nemáte rohlíky?                     Do you have rohlíky?

  Nechceš jít dnes do kina?           Would you like to go to movies?
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------

#### Assurances

  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- ------------------
  That's OK.        To je v pořádku.  There\'s no       To nespěchá.
                                      hurry.            

  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- ------------------

#### Female forms of occupation names

  -------------------------- ----------- ------------------------------
  učitel, řidič, fotograf,   **-ka**     učitelka, řidička, fotografka,
  ředitel,                               ředitelka

  dělník, úředník            **-ice**    dělnice, úřednice

  soudce, důchodce           **-kyně**   soudkyně, důchodkyně

  vrátný                     **-ý \>     vrátná
                             -á**        

  průvodčí, vrchní           **-í \>     průvodčí, vrchní
                             -í**        
  -------------------------- ----------- ------------------------------

In Czech, most nouns for occupations have both a male and a female form.
The female form is typically formed from the male form.

The *vrátný/vrátná* and *vrchní* models are conjugated as adjectives.

Two professions in particular are so commonly associated with females
that they have no easy male equivalent. They are *zdravotní sestra*
(nurse) and *sekretářka* (secretary). A male secretary could be
*asistent* (although there is *asistentka*) or *tajemník* (which would
be used for the Secretary of the UN). *Sekretář* would be used for a
piece of furniture. There is no easy way of saying male nurse since
*sestra* means sister.

#### Formation of Czech last names 

All Czech women\'s surnames end in *--**ová*** and are conjugated as
adjectives (with the exception of those ending in ***-ů***).

  ----------------- -------- ----------------- ----------------- -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
  pan                        paní              manželé           ![](media/image92-wmf.png){width="0.19791666666666666in"
                                                                 height="0.16041666666666668in"} rodina

  Novák             +ová     Novák**ová**      Novák**ovi+**     Novák**ova++**

  Čapek             +ová     Čapk**ová**\*     Čapk**ovi**       Čapk**ova**

  Frýba             +ová     Frýb**ová**       Frýb**ovi**       Frýb**ova**

  Novotn**ý**\*\*   ý **\>** Novotn**á**       Novotn**í**       Novotn**ých**
                    á                                            

  Jank**ů**         ů        Jank**ů**         Jank**ů**         Jank**ů**\*\*\*
  ----------------- -------- ----------------- ----------------- -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

\* C**e**C + ová **\>** CCová

\*\* These surnames have the form of an adjective and they are
conjugated using the hard adjective model.

\*\*\* Surnames ending in --ů are rare and are mostly derived from first
names like Petrů, Karlů, Janů. They do not change their form. Equivalent
surnames without the --ů also exist: Petr-Petrová-Petrovi.

\+ These forms are conjugated according to the hard adjective plural
model.

++ These forms are conjugated according to the possessive adjective
model.

#### Liking in Czech (*Mám rád* \... a *Líbí se mi \...)*

##### Like things and people

In Czech, there are two ways of expressing your likes. You can either
like the way something looks *líbit se* or like it for its properties
*mít rád*. The difference is also grammatical. With *mít rád* the person
who likes is the subject and the liked person or thing is the object
(*Petr má rád Martinu* - Petr likes Martina). While with *líbit se* the
liked person or thing is the subject and the person who likes is an
indirect object (*Komu? Čemu?* \[dative\] case-see below). E.g. *Líbí se
mi Praha -* I like Praha \[*Praha is pleasing to me*-I like the way it
looks\].

  ----------------- ---------------- ------------------------------------
  **mít rád\        **líbit se komu\ 
  + Koho? Co?**     + Kdo? Co?**     

  mám rád Janu      líbí se mi Jana  I love (like) Jana vs. I like the
                                     way Jana looks

  mám rád rohlíky   líbí se mi       because they taste good *vs*.
                    rohlíky          because they look funny

  mám rád knihy     líbí se mi knihy because they are interesting *vs.*
                                     because they look nice
  ----------------- ---------------- ------------------------------------

*mít rád* is more general, and is never used with specific objects

  ----------------- ---------------- ------------------------------------
  mám rád ten       líbí se mi ten   I\'m in love with this *rohlík* vs.
  rohlík            rohlík           I like the way it looks

  ----------------- ---------------- ------------------------------------

##### Like food (chutnat)

If you want to say you like the food in front of you, you have to use
*chutnat*; it has the same structure as *líbit se*

  ----------------- ---------------- ------------------------------------
  ten rohlík mi     ten rohlík se mi I like this roll (tastes good) vs. I
  chutná            líbí             like it (it looks good)

  ----------------- ---------------- ------------------------------------

##### Like to do

Here, you only use *rád* and the form of the verb.

**Note:** Rád has to reflect the gender and number of the subject:
**rád** (man), **ráda** (woman), **rádo** (child), **rádi** (plural)**,
rády** (plural women or children)

  ------------------------ ----------------------------------------------
  **rád** + verb (NOT      
  infinitive)              

  rád čtu                  I like to read

  rád hraju tenis          I like to play tennis

  rád dělám úkoly          I like to do homework
  ------------------------ ----------------------------------------------

##### Negating *rád*

The word rád is negated differently if it is used about activities vs
when it is used about people or objects. With *mám rád, ne* goes on the
the verb *mít*. With rád + Verb, *ne* goes on *rád*.

  ------------------------ ----------------------------------------------
  mám rád knihy            nemám rád knihy

  rád čtu                  nerad čtu
  ------------------------ ----------------------------------------------

#### *Prosím* in Czech -- the multipurpose conversation glue

  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  Zavři okno, *prosím.*               Close the window, **please.**

  Kávu, *prosím*. / *Prosím* jednu    Coffee, **please**. / One coffee
  kávu                                **please**.

  (Děkuju.) \> *Prosím.*              (Thank you.) **\>** **You\'re
                                      welcome.**

  *Prosím.* (Tady je ta kniha.)       **Here you are.** (Here\'s the
                                      book.)

  Prosím Tě.                          **Please.** (Informal)

  Prosím Vás.                         **Please.** (Formal)
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------

*Prosím* is either used as a particle or a verb. If used as a verb it is
followed by a noun in the Koho? Co? case, e.g. *Kávu prosím*. It is the
*I* form of the verb *prosit* (to ask, to beg, to plead).

#### prý, prej - I hear, they say

There is this little word *prý* (with its colloquial partner *prej*)
which is used to report on things that you have heard from an
unspecified source and may not be sure whether they're true or not. It
may be translated as *I hear* or *They say* into English. For example:

**Prý** bude letos drahý benzín. (I hear that gas will be expensive this
year.)

#### How do you address people?

This is an issue of politeness. It is very important to know how to
address people.

- Rule 1: The ending of a name is changed when addressing the person
  (Micha[l]{.underline} - Michale).

- Rule 2: In polite conversations, it is important to use the title if
  the person has one. Very much like in German. The name is not used
  after the title when addressing.

#### Going to see someone

Czech has two prepositions (similar to the French *chez*) that are
usually translated into English as *going to see someone* or *being at
someone's place.*

+-----------------------------------+----------------------------------+
| **u (being at someone's)**        | **k (going to see someone)**     |
+-----------------------------------+----------------------------------+
| Dnes večer budeme u Katky.        | Zítra půjdeme k babičce.         |
|                                   |                                  |
| (Tonight, we'll be at Katka's.)   | (Tomorrow, we'll go to see       |
|                                   | granny.)                         |
| Kde jsi? U zubaře.                |                                  |
|                                   | Musím jít k doktorovi.           |
| (Where are you? At the dentists.) |                                  |
|                                   | (I must go see a doctor.)        |
+-----------------------------------+----------------------------------+

#### Small talk

There is no expression in Czech to describe the English 'small talk'.
There is a word for 'chitchat' (pokec) or you can say 'Přijďte si
popovídat' (Come over for a chat). But there is no word for 'small
talk'. Sentences like 'they engaged in meaningless small talk' or 'she
is no good at small talk' are almost impossible translate into Czech.

Czechs have many fewer tools to engaged in small talk. There is no
equivalent of the 'how are you' although that may be changing under the
influence of American TV. Some phrases you can use on meeting people
casually without necessarily wanting to know how they are:

- Jak se vede? (How is it going)

- (Tak) jak to jde? (How is it going)

- Jak se daří? (How is it going?)

The response to these is something like:

- Ale jde to. (Not bad)

- No jo, to víte. Nestěžuju si. (You know. I don't complaint.)

However, these phrases are not quite as formalized as they are in
English.

Asking about other people is also common:

- A co máma? (How about your mom?)

- Jak se daří mámě?

- Jak se vede bratrovi? (How is it going with your brother)

A common noncommital answer is:

- Ale jo, jde to. (Not bad.)

- No to víte, jak by to šlo. (You know, how could it be)

However, these are all rather informal and quite loose. A conversation
can be built up from these blocks for a while.

#### Wishing good luck, trip, nice time, etc.

  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- ------------------
  Have a nice       Hezký víkend.     Have a nice trip. Šťastnou cestu.
  weekend.                                              

  Have a good time. Dobře se bavte.   Good luck.        Hodně štěstí.

  Enjoy yourself.   Užijte si to.     Fingers crossed.  Držím ti palce.
  ----------------- ----------------- ----------------- ------------------

#### What are the acronyms of academic titles used with people's names?

Czechs use their academic titles with their names even outside strictly
professional contexts. Czech uses a variety of acronyms that can precede
or be appended to a person's name. These are the most common titles
ranked more or less by importance.

  ----------- ---------------------- ----------- --------------------------------
  **Mgr.**    magistr -- MA          **RNDr.**   doktor -- sciences
                                                 specialization

  **Ing.**    inženýr -- MSc         **CSc.**    kandidát věd --after name, not
                                                 awarded any more

  **PhDr.**   doktor -- PhD, general **DrSc.**   doktor věd --after name, not
                                                 awarded any more

  **JUDr.**   doktor -- legal        **Doc.**    docent -- associate professor
              specialization                     independent of a school

  **MUDr.**   doktor -- medical      **Prof.**   profesor - professor independent
              doctor                             of a school
  ----------- ---------------------- ----------- --------------------------------

### Text and conversation structure

#### a vs. i / and vs. as well

There is a significant differrence between *i* and *a*. \'i\' means
basically \'as well as\' while \'a\' means \'and\'. Sometimes their
meanings overlap slightly but there\'s always a subtle change in
perspective.

  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------
  **a**                               **i**

  Byl tam Petr a Pavel                Byl tam Petr i Pavel

  Petr and Pavel were there.          Petr and Pavel were both there.
  ----------------------------------- -----------------------------------

When *i* occurs before a noun, it means *even* or *as well*: \'I Petr
tam byl\' - \'Even Petr showed up\'/\'Petr was there, as well\'

You can even have *a* and *i* as part of the same clause in spoken
language: \'A i Petr tam byl\' - \'And Petr was there, too.\'

#### Conditional with the infinitive

Sometimes in colloquial speech, the first part of a conditional sentence
can be expressed through the infinitive.

**Vědět to**, nešel bych tam. If I had known it, I wouldn't have gone
there.

**Být vámi**, nedělal bych to. If I were you, I wouldn't do it.

#### Telling stories about sequences of events

To tell a story, a lot of connecting words are needed:

  ------------- --------------------------- ------------------------------
  **teď**       Teď sedím doma a čtu si.    **Now** I'm sitting at home
                                            and I'm reading.

  **předtím**   Předtím jsem se díval na    **Before that** I was watching
                televizi.                   TV.

  **potom**     Potom si uvařím večeři.     **After that** I will cook
                                            dinner.

  **později**   Později se půjdu na         **Later**, I will go for a
                procházku.                  walk.

  **nakonec**   Nakonec půjdu spát.         **Finally** (at the end), I
                                            will go to bed.
  ------------- --------------------------- ------------------------------

### Strong and taboo language

#### Animal insults

  --------- ---------- ---------------------------------------------------
  prase     pig        dirty (not used for eating too much)

  prasák    pigman     dirty (sexually suggestive) man

  svině     swine      bastard, asshole (not very strong but extremely
                       informal)

  kráva     cow        bitch (stronger than cow in British English)

  vůl       ox         moron (quite strong)

  tele      calf       idiot, clueless, naive

  kůň       horse      stupid (man) (not very strong and not very common)

  slepice   hen        dismissive insult used to describe a woman

  husa      goose      stupid (woman) -- used often by women about women

  koza      goat       stupid (woman) -- used mostly by women (often self
                       directed)

  kozel     billy goat randy old goat

  ovce      sheep      easily led (same as English)

  osel      donkey     stupid (man)
  --------- ---------- ---------------------------------------------------

#### How do you swear in Czech? 

The short answer is YOU don't. In English, the acceptability of the most
common swear words has increased greatly in the last few decades. While
still considered inappropriate for children and mainstream broadcasting,
they are more and more acceptable in many contexts. There are no such
swearwords in Czech.

The most common Czech expletives have little to do with sex. While there
are many sex-related taboo words, the common expletives have more to do
with excreta. The following list gives the most common expletives and
related euphemisms. Learners are strongly advised not to use them in any
context.

+----------------+----------------+-----------------------------------+
| Expletive      | Euphemisms     | English explanation               |
+================+================+===================================+
| do prdele      | do Prčic       | Expression of frustration.        |
|                |                | Literally: "to ass"               |
|                | do prkýnka     |                                   |
+----------------+----------------+-----------------------------------+
| kurva          | kurnik         | Stronger expression of            |
|                |                | frustration. Probably the closest |
|                | kurňa          | to exclaiming Fuuck! in           |
|                |                | furstration. Literally: "whore".  |
|                |                | When used as an insult, can be    |
|                |                | directed at men.                  |
+----------------+----------------+-----------------------------------+
| jdi do prdele  | jdi do Prčic   | Expression of frustration         |
|                |                | directed at another person.       |
|                | jdi se bodnout | Literally: "go to ass"            |
+----------------+----------------+-----------------------------------+
| je to na hovno | je to na houby | Dismissive expletive indicating   |
|                |                | low value of an object or idea.   |
| stojí to za    |                | Literally: "shit". **Note:** this |
| hovno          |                | is never used as an expresion of  |
|                |                | frustration.                      |
+----------------+----------------+-----------------------------------+
| já se na to    | já se na to    | Expressing dissatisfaction with   |
| vyseru         | vykašlu        | something or giving up on         |
|                |                | something. Roughly equivalent to  |
|                |                | *fuck it*, *I give up* but can    |
|                |                | also be used to describe someone  |
|                |                | not finishing something.          |
|                |                | Literally *shit on something* or  |
|                |                | in the euphemism *cough on        |
|                |                | something*.                       |
+----------------+----------------+-----------------------------------+
| posraný        | podělaný       | Adjective describing something    |
|                |                | negatively. Roughly equivalent to |
|                |                | *fucking* or *fucked up*. Lit.    |
|                |                | *shat on*.                        |
+----------------+----------------+-----------------------------------+

#### Dude! -- ty vole

Even though *vůl* (ox) is an insult, in its Vocative form, *vole*, it
can also be used to mean something equivalent to *dude*. You can often
hear people sprinkle their speech (when addressing a very good friend)
with *ty vole*. This is not an insult but is also completely
inappropriate for any context outside extremely informal interactions
with friends. For example, it would not be used within the family
context.

Some people use *vole* or *ty vole* as a filler in their informal speech
similarly to the way people overuse *you know* or *you see* in English.

#### Religious expletives and euphemisms

Expletives based on religion are no longer perceived as problematic. But
there are still many old euphemisms that are used even if nobody
perceives the full religious expletives to be blasphemous. They all have
roughly the same meaning of expressing frustration and dismay.

  ------------------------------ ----------------------------
  Ježíši Kriste                  Jesus

  Ježišmarjá                     Jesus Mary

  pane Bože                      Lord

  panenko Marie                  virgin Mary

  hergot, herdek, hernajz        from German Herr Gott

  kruci, krucinál, kruci písek   from Latin for cross

  sakra, sakryš, sakra práce,    from sacral
  sakramente                     

  pro pána Jána, pro pána Krále  for Lord John's/King's sake

  mankote, šmankote              from German mein Gott
  ------------------------------ ----------------------------

#### Sex and genitalia taboo words and insults

Strong language related to sex or human genitalia is entirely
inappropriate for learners. There are no neutral terms that would be
acceptable in conversation and we strongly advise against attempting to
use them even in jest if you look them up in a dictionary.

## Differences between standard and non-standard Czech

As is usual in most languages, the Czech colloquial language has
different pronunciation, and specific words and expressions. Unlike most
languages, in Czech, the colloquial language also uses consistently
different endings. For instance, you learn *mlad[ý]{.underline}* (young)
and hear *mlad[e]{.underline}j* on the street, or you see *Šumavský
chléb* (Šumava bread) written on a shelf in the bakery but people ask
for *Šumavskej chleba*. As you see the difference is mostly in vowels.

It is also noteworthy that the Moravian \'dialect\' uses many of the
endings of the written standard, although not consistently, leading many
Moravians to claim that they speak "better Czech" than Bohemians.

### Which 'Czech' is taught to foreigners?

As a learner you don\'t have to worry too much about it, but it is
important to keep in mind that you may hear some endings pronounced
differently from how you learned them.

Choosing the right form can be a hard decision for a teacher to make.
The standard variant is more regular but also more complex. If you
started speaking colloquial Czech with beginners\' mistakes it would
sound ridiculous. The solution usually is, to use a simplified standard
Czech and make it more communicative by introducing elements from the
colloquial as appropriate.

The trick is that the two \'languages\' are not always mutually
exclusive. For two words in one sentence uttered by a Czech in normal
everyday communication, you may find one ending colloquial and one
literary, and there are no rules for this. Therefore, teachers cannot
simply resolve to teach one or the other because except for in writing
they are rarely separated. On top of that, Czechs themselves sometimes
quarrel which is which.

The hard thing is to tell when to use which ending. The terms colloquial
and literary or spoken and written are not very accurate. The colloquial
variant is never used in writing (except, paradoxically enough, in
literature), or for official purposes on TV, radio announcements,
speeches, advertisements etc. You would use it with your friends but it
